are generators.

Proof.

Since the relations of
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
are all in genus zero, all the quadratic higher genus relations are in the orthogonal complement. Then, the four elements above generate all of those by symmetry.
∎

Theorem 2.21
.

The coproperad
(F\mathcal{Y}^{(n)})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\antishrieksymbol$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\antishrieksymbol$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\antishrieksymbol$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\antishrieksymbol$}}}
is a codioperad.

Proof.

Let us fix
n
and denote
\mathrm{BIB}=\mathrm{BIB}^{1-n}
. By
Proposition

2.18
it is sufficient to prove that
(F\mathcal{Y}^{(n)})^{!}
is trivial in higher genus, or in other words, that the submodule
\langle R^{\perp}\rangle
contains every element given by a graph of genus
\geqslant 1
. We already know that
R^{\perp}
contains every higher-genus graph with exactly two internal vertices, so we consider from now on graphs that have at least three internal vertices. Since
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)!}
is generated by
\mathrm{BIB}^{!}
and an extra generator
\psi
, every such graph is given by attaching
\psi
vertices, of arity
(3,0)
, to collections of
\mathrm{BIB}^{!}
-graphs, such as:

\mathrm{BIB}^{!}
\mathrm{BIB}^{!}
\mathrm{BIB}^{!}
\psi
\psi
\psi

By
[
19
, Corollary 24]
, every
\mathrm{BIB}^{!}
-graph with genus
\geqslant 1
is zero, so it is enough to look at diagrams as above where each
\mathrm{BIB}^{!}
-subgraph is a tree, which we can put in planar form. If the graph has nonzero genus, there must be a cycle with
k\geqslant 1
vertices
\psi
. The
k
strands connecting these
\psi
vertices have sequences of
\omega
and
\nu
vertices. Since there can be no internal sink or vertices, that is, vertices with no outgoing edges, each of the
k
strands must have at least one
\nu
vertex. We then use the relations labeled (2) to (5) in our calculation of
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)!}
above to ‘bring’ each such
\nu
vertex to be adjacent to a
\psi
vertex, getting a cycle that looks like

\psi
\nu
\psi
\nu
\psi
\nu

…

\dots
\dots

…

Note that this graph may not be embedded in the plane as in the example above. In any case, we can use relation (7) to eliminate all
\psi
vertices from the cycle. Repeating the procedure until all cycles are gone, we get to a single
\mathrm{BIB}^{!}
-graph, possibly with
\psi
vertices attached to it by a single edge, that is, not forming any cycles of the form above. Note that none of the relations changes the topology of the underlying graph, therefore the resulting
\mathrm{BIB}^{!}
-graph has the same genus
\geqslant 1
we started with, and vanishes by the aforementioned result.
∎

Remark 2.22
.

By
Proposition

2.18
and
Theorem

2.21
, we have equivalent categories of (dioperadic)
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebras and (properadic)
(F\mathcal{Y}^{(n)})_{\infty}
-algebras. We will, from now on, elide this distinction and just use
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
to also refer to the properad, and work purely in the setting of properadic algebras.

2.6.
Pre-CY and
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-structures on based loop spaces

Let us describe the class of
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebras that will be our main source of examples. For that, we recall the relation between pre-Calabi–Yau and Calabi–Yau structures from
[
12
]
. Let
(A,\mu)
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra over
R
, which is
smooth
, in the sense that its diagonal bimodule
A
is perfect; in that case, we have a quasi-isomorphism

CH_{*}(A)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{R}\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A-A}(A^{!},A)

between Hochschild chains and the derived bimodule morphisms between the inverse dualizing bimodule
A^{!}
and the diagonal bimodule
A
. We recall that there is a canonical map
HC^{-}_{*}(A)\to HH_{*}(A)
from negative cyclic to Hochschild homology.

Definition 2.23
.

A (smooth)
n
-Calabi–Yau structure
on
A
is a negative cyclic homology class

[\omega]\in HC^{-}_{n}(A)

whose image under the map above gives a quasi-isomorphism of bimodules
A^{!}\xrightarrow{\sim}A
.

One should regard a smooth
n
-Calabi–Yau structure as something like a noncommutative
n
-shifted symplectic form. We now paraphrase a result of
[
12
]
, which upgrades the usual relation between symplectic forms and nondegenerate Poisson bivectors to this noncommutative level. The role of Poisson bivector is played by an element of
CH^{*}_{(2)}(A)
. If
\alpha
is a closed element of degree
n
in the complex

(CH^{*}_{(2)}(A),[\mu,-])\ ,

we have a map of complexes

g_{\alpha}\colon CH_{*}(A)\to CH^{-*}(A)[n]

given by evaluating a certain diagram, which is an analog of the map between forms and vector fields induced by a bivector.

Definition 2.24
.

Let
A
be a smooth
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
[\omega_{0}]\in HH_{n}(A)
be a Hochschild homology class. We say that an element

\varphi_{(2)}\in CH^{n}_{(2)}(A)

is
compatible
with
[\omega_{0}]
if it satisfies the equation
[g_{\varphi_{(2)}}(\omega_{0})]\cong 1
in
HH^{0}(A)
.

Theorem 2.25

(
[
12
, Proposition 4.2 and Theorem 4.7]
)
.

For any smooth
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
and nondegenerate Hochschild homology class
[\omega_{0}]\in HH_{n}(A)
, there is a bijection between the following sets:

(1)

the set of lifts of
[\omega_{0}]
to a smooth
n
-CY structure, that is, to a negative cyclic class
[\omega]
in its preimage under the map
HC^{-}_{*}(A)\to HH_{*}(A)
, and

(2)

\pi_{0}
of the space of
n
-pre-CY structures
\varphi
on
A
whose component

\varphi_{(2)}\in CH^{n}_{(2)}(A)

is compatible with
\omega_{0}
.

Remark 2.26
.

In the theorem above, it is essential that the space of pre-CY structures be modeled as a certain simplicial space. We note that in this context the notion of ‘equivalence’, that is, being in the same connected component, is more general than the notion of gauge equivalence. For more details see the discussion in section 4 of
op.cit.

One source of such smooth Calabi–Yau structures is the algebra of chains on based loop spaces of
local Poincaré duality pairs
, that is, topological spaces with a fundamental class giving Poincaré duality with local system coefficients. Let
X
be any path-connected space with the homotopy type of a finite simplicial complex, and denote
A=C_{*}(\Omega X,R)
; this is a dg algebra, whose product is induced by concatenation of based loops.

Theorem 2.27

(
[
10
]
)
.

Let
R
be any ring and
[X]\in H_{n}(X,R)
any degree
n
class, represented by some
n
-chain
\alpha_{X}
. If
(X,[X])
is a local Poincaré duality pair of dimension
n
, in the sense that
\frown\alpha_{X}
induces a quasi-isomorphism

C^{*}(X,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{n-*}(X,\mathcal{L})

for any local system
\mathcal{L}
, then
\alpha_{X}
induces a smooth Calabi–Yau structure on
A=C_{*}(\Omega X,R)
. Moreover, the class of this smooth CY structure in negative cyclic homology only depends on
[X]
and not on the chosen representative
\alpha_{X}
.

Remark 2.28
.

The theorem above generalizes previous results, in the case of manifolds, of
[
1
,
4
]
.

Definition 2.29
.

Let
(X,[X])
be a local Poincaré duality pair of dimension
n
. We say that an
n
-pre-CY algebra structure
\varphi
on
A=C_{*}(\Omega X,R)
is
compatible
with
[X]
if
\varphi
corresponds to the smooth CY structure of
Theorem

2.27
under the bijection of
Theorem

2.25
.

Recall that
n
-pre-CY structures are algebra structures over the dg dioperad
\mathcal{V}^{(n)}_{\infty}
, which is a cofibrant resolution of the dioperad
\mathcal{V}^{(n)}
of
[
18
]
. As mentioned in the introduction, we use the notion of formality discussed in
[
6
, Section 2]
. If
\mathcal{C}
is a weight-graded reduced cooperad/codioperad/coproperad, there is a morphism of operads/dioperads/properads

\Omega\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}=\left.\mathcal{T}(s^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{(1)})\middle/d_{\Omega\mathcal{C}}(s^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{(2)})\right.

and any
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structure on a complex induces a
\mathcal{P}
-structure on its homology. One can define formality of such structures in the following sense.

Definition 2.30
.

[
6
, Definition 2.5]

A
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra
(A,\varphi)
is said to be
formal
if there is a zig-zag of quasi-isomorphisms of
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebras

(A,\varphi)\overset{\sim}{\longleftarrow}\cdot\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\cdots\overset{\sim}{\longleftarrow}\cdot\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(H_{*}(A),\varphi_{*})\ .

A graded
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
(H,\varphi_{*})
is said to be
intrinsically formal
if, for any chain complex
A
with
H_{*}(A)=H
and
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structure
\varphi
on
A
inducing
\varphi_{*}
on
H
, the
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra
(A,\varphi)
is formal.

We could consider the setting where the morphism given by the cofibrant resolution of dioperads

\mathcal{V}^{(n)}_{\infty}=\Omega(\mathcal{V}^{(n)})^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\antishrieksymbol$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\antishrieksymbol$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\antishrieksymbol$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\antishrieksymbol$}}}\to\mathcal{V}^{(n)}.

However, it turns out that for the class of algebras we are interested in, the question of formality as
\mathcal{V}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebras is not interesting, due to the following result.

Theorem 2.31
.

Let
(X,[X])
be a local Poincaré duality pair of dimension
n\geq 1
. None of the
n
-pre-CY algebra structures on
A=C_{*}(\Omega X,R)
that are compatible with
[X]
are formal.

Proof.

Consider any
n
-pre-CY whose
m_{(2)}
component is compatible with the smooth CY structure. Compatibility implies that the class of
m_{(2)}
is nonzero in
HH^{n}_{(2)}(A)
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
(A,m)
is formal as a
\mathcal{V}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra, that is, that there is a zig-zag of
\mathcal{V}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphisms between
(A,m)
and its homology with induced
\mathcal{V}^{(n)}
-structure
(H,n)
. In particular, this implies that

(1)

A
is formal as an
A_{\infty}
-algebra, by giving a zig-zag of
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphisms between
(A,m_{(1)})
and
(H,n_{(1)})
, and

(2)

the classes
[m_{(2)}]\in HH^{n}_{(2)}(A)
and
[n_{(2)}]\in HH^{n}_{(2)}(H)
are identified by the isomorphism on Hochschild homology induced by this zig-zag.

However, since
n\geq 1
and
H
is concentrated in non-negative homological degree, the element
n_{(2)}
vanishes, contradicting the fact that
[m_{(2)}]
is nonzero.
∎

As a result, it would not make much sense to define coformality of local Poincaré pairs in terms of
\mathcal{V}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures. We propose the setting of
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures as a better setting for this definition. We first identify which structures to associate to a pair.

Definition 2.32
.

Let
(X,[X])
be a local Poincaré pair. A
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structure
\varphi
on
A=C_{*}(\Omega X,R)
is
compatible
with
[X]
if it corresponds under
Proposition

2.17
to a compatible
n
-pre-CY structure. Likewise, a
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structure on
H_{*}(A)=H_{*}(\Omega X,R)
is
compatible
with
[X]
if it is induced by a compatible
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
A
.

Proposition 2.33
.

Let
(X,[X])
be a local Poincaré duality pair of dimension
n\geqslant 1
. Then there exist
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
A=C_{*}(\Omega X,R)
that are compatible with
[X]
.

Proof.

This proposition follows from
Theorem

2.25
and
Proposition

2.17
.
∎

We now define coformality of a local Poincaré pair in terms of
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures.

Definition 2.34
.

Let
(X,[X])
be a local Poincaré duality pair of degree
n\geqslant 1
. We say that the pair
(X,[X])
is

•

coformal
over
R
when all
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
A=C_{*}(\Omega X,R)
that are compatible with
[X]
are formal as
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebras, and

•

intrinsically coformal
over
R
when all
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structures on
H_{*}(A)=H_{*}(\Omega X,R)
that are compatible with
[X]
are intrinsically formal as
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebras.

In fact, when assessing intrinsic coformality, it will often be sufficient for us to study a single
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structure, due to the following fact.

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
be two
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
A=C_{*}(\Omega X,R)
that are compatible with
[X]
. Then their induced
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structures
\varphi_{*}
and
\varphi^{\prime}_{*}
on
H_{*}(A)=H_{*}(\Omega X,R)
are equal.

Proof.

We note that the
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structure on
H_{*}(A)
induced by any
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structure
\varphi
on
A
only depends on the component
(\varphi_{(2)})^{1,0}
with one input and two outputs. If
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
are any two compatible
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures, their components
\varphi_{(2)}
and
\varphi^{\prime}_{(2)}
must be compatible with the same class in
HH_{n}(A)
; so by
[
12
, Proposition 2.2]
they represent the same class in
HH^{n}_{(2)}(A)
. In other words, there is some element
\psi\in CH^{n-1}_{(2)}(A)
such that

\varphi^{\prime}_{(2)}-\varphi_{(2)}=d\psi

However, since
n\geqslant 2
and
A
is supported in non-negative degrees, the component without outputs
\psi^{0,0}
vanishes and
(\varphi_{(2)})^{1,0}=(\varphi^{\prime}_{(2)})^{1,0}
.
∎

Remark 2.36
.

In
Proposition

2.33
, the assumption of characteristic zero is necessary due to the inductive procedure used and the symmetrization at each step used in
[
12
]
. Moreover, over an arbitrary commutative ring
R
, even when there are compatible
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
A
, they might not induce
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structures on homology, due to the failure of the Künneth map to be an isomorphism.

Since the dg Lie algebra governing
A_{\infty}
-structures sits as a subalgebra of the one governing
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures, we have the following result.

Proposition 2.37
.

Let
(X,[X])
be a space with degree
n\geqslant 1
Poincaré duality with local coefficients that has an induced
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structure
\varphi_{*}
on
H=H_{*}(\Omega X,R)
. If it is coformal, then
X
is coformal in the sense that the dg algebra
C_{*}(\Omega X,R)
is formal as an
A_{\infty}
-algebra.

3.
Intermediate obstruction sequences to formality

In
[
7
]
, the first-named author constructs obstruction sequences to formality of algebras encoded by properads, such as pre-CY algebras with vanishing copairing. In
Section

4
, we will compute these obstructions to study pre-CY coformality of spheres. To this end, we will exploit the existence of an additional filtration; in this section, we explain the general framework for refining obstruction sequences in the presence of that extra filtration.

3.1.
Intermediate gauge triviality sequences

We will work with the following assumptions.

Assumptions 1
.

(1)

Let
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-])
be a weight-graded Lie algebra over
R
, i.e. a complete Lie algebra (with vanishing differential) with an additional weight grading such that

\mathfrak{g}\cong\prod_{k\geqslant 1}\mathfrak{g}^{(k)},\quad\left[\mathfrak{g}^{(k)},\mathfrak{g}^{(l)}\right]\subset\mathfrak{g}^{(k+l)}.

We denote by
\cdot
the gauge group action and the canonical projections by

\pi_{k}:\mathfrak{g}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{g}/\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{g},\ \text{where}\ \mathcal{F}^{n}\mathfrak{g}\coloneqq\prod_{k\geqslant n}\mathfrak{g}^{(k)}.

(2)

Suppose that
\mathfrak{g}
has an an additional descending filtration

\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{L}^{0}\mathfrak{g}\supset\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathfrak{g}\supset\mathcal{L}^{2}\mathfrak{g}\supset\cdots

that is compatible with the Lie bracket, in the sense that
[\mathcal{L}^{i}\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{L}^{j}\mathfrak{g}]\subset\mathcal{L}^{i+j}\mathfrak{g}
for any
i,j\geqslant 0
, and that is bounded with respect to
\mathcal{F}
, that is, for every
k\geqslant 1
, there exists
\delta_{k}
such that
\mathcal{L}^{\delta_{k}}\mathfrak{g}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{g}
.

Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be two Maurer–Cartan elements in
\mathfrak{g}
, such that
\psi
is of homogeneous weight one. Let us set
\mathfrak{h}
for the dg Lie algebra twisted by
\psi

\mathfrak{h}\coloneqq\left(\mathfrak{g},[-,-],d^{\psi}\coloneqq[\psi,-],\mathcal{F}\right)\ .

We want to detect whether
\varphi
and
\psi
are gauge equivalent, or equivalently, whether
\phi\coloneqq\varphi-\psi
is
gauge trivial
, i.e. if there exists
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
such that
\lambda\cdot\phi=0
. By
[
7
, Section 1]
, even without the additional filtration
\mathcal{L}
, this can be achieved through the construction of a
gauge triviality sequence

\{\vartheta_{k}\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\ ,

which is either an infinite sequence of vanishing homology classes, when
n=\infty
; or a finite sequence of trivial classes that ends on a nonvanishing class
\vartheta_{n}
for some
n\geqslant 1
. This index
n
of the last class only depends on
\phi
and is called the
gauge triviality degree
of
\phi

[
7
, Theorem 1.17]
. This gauge triviality sequence is constructed by induction: we first set

\phi_{1}\coloneqq\phi\quad\mbox{and}\quad\vartheta_{1}\coloneqq\left[\pi_{2}(\phi_{1})\right]\in H_{-1}\left(\mathfrak{h}/\mathcal{F}^{2}\mathfrak{h}\right)\ .

Let us suppose that
\phi_{i}
and
\vartheta_{i}
have been constructed for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant k

\centerdot

If
\vartheta_{k}\neq 0
, then
k
is the gauge triviality degree of
\phi
, and we stop.

\centerdot

If
\vartheta_{k}=0
, there must exist
\upsilon_{k}\in\mathfrak{h}
such that
\upsilon_{k}\cdot\phi_{k}\in\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
. We set

\phi_{k+1}\coloneqq\upsilon_{k}\cdot\phi_{k}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\vartheta_{k+1}\coloneqq\left[\pi_{k+2}(\phi_{k+1})\right]\in H_{-1}\left(\mathfrak{h}/\mathcal{F}^{k+2}\mathfrak{h}\right),

and we can continue to the next value of
k
.

It can be difficult to determine in practice whether the class
\vartheta_{k}
vanishes or not. This is where having an additional filtration will help.

Proposition 3.1
.

Under Assumptions
1
, let
\xi
be a Maurer–Cartan element in
\mathfrak{h}
such that

\xi\in\mathcal{L}^{i}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}

for some
i\geqslant 0
and
k\geqslant 2
. Let us consider its class in the homology of the quotient

\vartheta_{k}^{i}\coloneqq[\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\xi)]\in H_{-1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\right).

The following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

The homology class
\vartheta_{k}^{i}
vanishes.

(2)

There exists
\upsilon\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
such that
\upsilon\cdot\xi\in\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
.

Proof.

For all
\upsilon\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
, the gauge action formula gives

(1)

\upsilon\cdot\xi\equiv\xi-\frac{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\upsilon}}-\mathrm{id}}{\mathrm{ad}_{\upsilon}}(d^{\psi}\upsilon)\pmod{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\ .

If
\vartheta_{k}^{i}=0
, then there exists
\upsilon\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
such that

\xi\equiv d^{\psi}\upsilon\pmod{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\ .

Since
\xi\in\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
, this implies that
d^{\psi}\upsilon\in\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
and
\upsilon\in\mathcal{F}^{k-1}\mathfrak{h}
. Since
k\geqslant 2
, we have

\frac{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\upsilon}}-\mathrm{id}}{\mathrm{ad}_{\upsilon}}(d^{\psi}\upsilon)\equiv d^{\psi}\upsilon\pmod{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\ .

Equation

1
implies that
\upsilon\cdot\xi\in\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}\ .
Conversely, if point (2) holds, we have

\xi\equiv\frac{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{\upsilon}}-\mathrm{id}}{\mathrm{ad}_{\upsilon}}(d^{\psi}\upsilon)\pmod{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}

by
Equation

1
. Since
\xi\in\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
, this implies that
d^{\psi}\upsilon\in\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
and

\xi\equiv d^{\psi}\upsilon\pmod{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\ ,

which leads to
\vartheta_{k}^{i}=0
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

If the obstruction class
\vartheta_{k}^{i}
is nonvanishing then so is the obstruction class
\vartheta_{k}
. Indeed, the former is the image of the latter under the map

H_{-1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\right)\to H_{-1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\right).

Construction 3.3
.

Let
\xi\in\mathrm{MC}(\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h})
for
k\geqslant 2
. We aim to detect whether there exists
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
such that
\lambda\cdot\xi\in\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
, or equivalently whether the obstruction class

\vartheta_{k}=\left[\pi_{k+2}(\phi_{k+1})\right]\in H_{-1}\left(\mathfrak{h}/\mathcal{F}^{k+2}\mathfrak{h}\right),

vanishes, see
[
7
, Proposition 1.6]
. Let us set
\xi_{0}\coloneqq\xi
and consider the first obstruction

\vartheta_{k}^{0}\coloneqq[\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\xi_{0})]\in H_{-1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\right).

\centerdot

If
\vartheta_{k}^{0}
is non-zero, then so is
\vartheta_{k}
.

\centerdot

If
\vartheta_{k}^{0}=0
, there exists
\upsilon_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
, such that
\upsilon_{0}\cdot\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
, by the implication
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
of
Proposition

3.1
.

If
\vartheta_{k}^{0}=0
, we set
\xi_{1}\coloneqq\upsilon_{0}\cdot\xi_{0}
and

\vartheta_{k}^{1}\coloneqq[\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\xi_{1})]\in H_{-1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathcal{L}^{2}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\right).

\centerdot

If
\vartheta_{k}^{1}
is non-zero, then so is
\vartheta_{k}
.

\centerdot

If
\vartheta_{k}^{1}=0
, there exists
\upsilon_{1}\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
, such that
\upsilon_{1}\cdot\xi_{1}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
, by the implication
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
of
Proposition

3.1
.

The construction of such obstruction classes can be performed higher up in a similar way. This leads to a sequence of classes
\left(\vartheta_{k}^{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\eta}
which is either

\centerdot

an infinite sequence of vanishing homology classes, when
\eta_{k}=\infty
, or

\centerdot

a finite sequence of trivial classes that ends on a nonzero class
\vartheta_{\eta}
, when
\eta_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 3.4
.

By Assumptions
1
, the filtration
\mathcal{L}
is bounded and there exists
\delta_{k+1}
such that

\mathcal{L}^{\delta_{k+1}}\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}\ .

In the case where
\eta\in\mathbb{N}
is finite, we have
\eta_{k}<\delta_{k+1}-1
. In the case where
\eta_{k}=\infty
, the construction starts to be trivial at the level
k=\delta_{k+1}-1
where
\xi_{k}\in\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\xi\in\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{h})
be a Maurer–Cartan element such that
\xi\in\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
for
k\geqslant 2
. A
k^{th}
-intermediate gauge triviality sequence
of
\xi
is an obstruction sequence

\left(\vartheta_{k}^{i}\right)_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\eta_{k}},\quad\eta_{k}\in\{0,\dots,\delta_{k+1},\infty\}\ ,

obtained through
Construction

3.3
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\xi\in\mathrm{MC}(\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h})
for
k\geqslant 2
. The index
\eta_{k}\in\{0,\dots,\delta_{k+1},\infty\}
of the last class of a
k^{th}
-intermediate gauge triviality sequence only depends on
\xi
, i.e. given

\left(\vartheta_{k}^{i}\right)_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\eta_{k}}\quad\quad\left(\vartheta_{k}^{{}^{\prime}i}\right)_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\eta_{k}^{\prime}}

two
k^{th}
-intermediate gauge triviality sequences, we have
\eta_{k}=\eta_{k}^{\prime}
. This element is called the
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality degree
of
\xi
.

Proof.

The proof is the same than the one of
[
7
, Lemma 1.9]
.
∎

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
\xi\in\mathrm{MC}(\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h})
for
k\geqslant 2
. The following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

The
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality degree of
\xi
is equal to
\infty
;

(2)

The obstruction
\vartheta_{k}=\left[\pi_{k+2}(\phi_{k+1})\right]\in H_{-1}\left(\mathfrak{h}/\mathcal{F}^{k+2}\mathfrak{h}\right),
vanishes;

(3)

There exists
\omega\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
such that
\omega\cdot\xi\in\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
.

Proof.

Let us prove the implication
(1)\Rightarrow(3)
. Let
\left(\vartheta_{k}^{i}\right)_{0\leqslant i\leqslant\eta_{k}}
be a
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality sequence of
\xi
and let us denote by
\left(\upsilon_{k}\right)
and
\left(\xi_{k}\right)
the associated sequence of gauges and Maurer–Cartan elements given by
Construction

3.3
. If
\eta_{k}=\infty
, it follows from the construction that the gauge

\omega\coloneqq\mathrm{BCH}(\upsilon_{\delta_{k+1}-1},\mathrm{BCH}(\cdots\mathrm{BCH}(\upsilon_{2},\upsilon_{1}))\cdots)\ ,

satisfies
\omega\cdot\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
, see
Remark

3.4
. The equivalence
(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)
is given by
[
7
, Proposition 1.6]
. The implication
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
follows from
Remark

3.2
. Indeed, if the
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality degree
\eta_{k}
of
\xi
is not equal to
\infty
and

\vartheta^{\eta_{k}}_{k}\neq 0\implies\vartheta_{k}\neq 0\ .\qed

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\phi\in\mathrm{MC}(\mathcal{F}^{2}\mathfrak{h})
and suppose that there exist
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
such that
\omega\cdot\phi
and
\omega^{\prime}\cdot\phi
are both in
\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
. Then, they have equal
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality degrees.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that the
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality degree of
\omega\cdot\phi
is
\eta_{k}\in\{0,1,\dots,\infty\}
, and that of
\omega\cdot\phi
is a finite number such that
\eta_{k}^{\prime}<\eta_{k}
. By construction, there exists
\lambda
such that
\lambda\cdot(\omega\cdot\phi)\in\mathcal{L}^{\eta_{k}^{\prime}+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
. But we can rewrite this element as

\lambda\cdot(\omega\cdot\phi)=\mathrm{BCH}(\lambda,\omega)\cdot\phi=\mathrm{BCH}(\mathrm{BCH}(\lambda,\omega),-\omega^{\prime})\cdot(\omega^{\prime}\cdot\phi)\ .

By
Proposition

3.1
, the
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality degree of
\omega^{\prime}\cdot\phi
must be strictly larger than
\eta_{k}^{\prime}
, which is impossible. The case
\eta_{k}<\eta_{k}^{\prime}
is treated analogously.
∎

Definition 3.9
.

Let
\phi\in\mathrm{MC}(\mathcal{F}^{2}\mathfrak{h})
be a Maurer–Cartan element and suppose that there exist
\omega\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
such that
\omega\cdot\phi\in\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
. The
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality degree

\phi
is defined and is the one of
\omega\cdot\phi
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Under Assumptions
1
, the following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

The Maurer–Cartan element
\varphi
is gauge equivalent to its first component
\varphi^{(1)}
.

(2)

The gauge triviality degree of
\phi\coloneqq\varphi-\varphi^{(1)}
is equal to
\infty
.

(3)

For all
k\geqslant 2
, the
k^{th}
-intermediate gauge triviality degree of
\phi
is defined, and equal to
\infty
.

Proof.

The equivalence
(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)
is given by
[
7
, Theorem 1.17]
. Let us prove the equivalence
(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)
. The
k^{th}
-intermediate gauge triviality degree is defined for all
k\geqslant 2
if and only if there exists
\omega_{k}\in\mathfrak{h}_{0}
such that
\omega_{k}\cdot\phi\in\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
. By
[
7
, Theorem 1.11]
, this is equivalent to the gauge triviality degree of
\phi
being infinite. Now, by
Theorem

3.7
, for each
k\geqslant 1
, the
(k+1)^{th}
-intermediate gauge triviality degree is defined if and only if the
k^{th}
-intermediate gauge triviality degree is defined and infinite.
∎

3.2.
Rigidity criteria

Let us recall the notion of rigidity of a Maurer–Cartan element.

Definition 3.11
.

A Maurer–Cartan element
\psi
concentrated in weight one is
rigid
when any of its deformations is gauge-trivial, that is, when any Maurer–Cartan element
\varphi
satisfying
\varphi^{(1)}=\psi
is gauge equivalent to
\psi
.

The obstruction theory of Maurer–Cartan solutions gives the following characterization.

Proposition 3.12

(
[
7
, Corollary 1.18]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a weight-graded dg Lie algebra, and
\psi
a Maurer–Cartan element
\psi
concentrated in weight one. If

\mathcal{F}^{2}H_{-1}(\mathfrak{g}^{\psi})=0

then
\psi
is automatically rigid.

In the presence of an extra filtration
\mathcal{L}
, we can refine the rigidity criterion above. We first consider the following notion of rigidity with respect to the extra filtration.

Definition 3.13
.

The element
\psi
is
\mathcal{L}^{m}
-rigid
for some
m\geqslant 0
if every
\phi\in\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{g}^{\psi})
satisfying
\phi\in\mathcal{F}^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}
is gauge-equivalent to some element in
\mathcal{L}^{m+1}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Under Assumptions
1
, if
\psi
is
\mathcal{L}^{0}
-rigid, and if the image of the map

\mathcal{F}^{2}H_{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi})\longrightarrow H_{-1}(\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}/\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi})

vanishes for every
i\geqslant 1
, then
\psi
is rigid.

Proof.

Let us consider some Maurer–Cartan element
\varphi
such that
\varphi^{(1)}=\psi
, and again denote
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}
. Since
\phi=\varphi-\psi\in\mathrm{MC}(\mathfrak{h})
is in
\mathcal{F}^{2}\mathfrak{h}
and
\psi
is
\mathcal{L}^{0}
-rigid, we can assume
\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{i}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
, for some
i\geqslant 1
and
k\geqslant 2
. The Maurer–Cartan equation then implies that

d^{\psi}\phi=-\frac{1}{2}[\phi,\phi]\equiv 0\pmod{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathfrak{h}},

so
[\phi]
is a class in
\mathcal{F}^{k}H_{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}\mathfrak{h})
. We now consider the maps

\mathcal{F}^{k}H_{-1}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}\mathfrak{h}\right)\to\mathcal{F}^{k}H_{-1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathfrak{h}}\right)\to\mathcal{F}^{k}H_{-1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\right)\xleftarrow{f}\mathcal{F}^{k}H_{-1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}\right).

The last group is where the
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality class
\vartheta_{k}^{i}
lives. But the image of this class in
\mathcal{F}^{k}H_{-1}(\mathfrak{h}/\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h})
is equal to the image of
[\phi]\in\mathcal{F}^{k}H_{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}\mathfrak{h})
, which vanishes by assumption. Therefore, to show that all classes
\vartheta_{k}^{i}
vanish, it is sufficient to show that the map
f
is injective. Suppose we have
[\alpha]\in\ker(f)
. Then we can find
\lambda
such that

d^{\psi}\lambda\equiv\alpha\pmod{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}}.

Let us set
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda^{(k-1)}+\lambda^{(k)}+\cdots
Since
d^{\psi}
is homogeneous of weight one, we must have

d^{\psi}\left(\lambda^{\prime}\right)\equiv\alpha\pmod{\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}},

since
\alpha
is in
\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}
, in other words,
\lambda^{\prime}
is a primitive of
\alpha
in
\mathfrak{h}/\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{h}+\mathcal{F}^{k+1}\mathfrak{h}
.
∎

3.3.
Application to formality of properadic algebras

Let us recall the approach of formality as a deformation problem, following
[
9
,
6
]
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a reduced weight-graded dg coproperad, and as in
Definition

2.34
we have the canonical morphism

\Omega\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}=\left.\mathcal{T}(s^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{(1)})\middle/d_{\Omega\mathcal{C}}(s^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{(2)})\right.

Any chain complex
A
and
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structure
\varphi
on
A
, its homology
H=H_{*}(A)
has two
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structures:

•

an
induced

\mathcal{P}
-algebra structure
\varphi_{*}
, in other words, a
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structure concentrated in weight one, and

•

a
transferred

\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structure
\varphi_{t}
whose weight one component agrees with
\varphi_{*}
and such that
(A,\varphi)
and
(H,\varphi_{t})
are quasi-isomorphic as
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebras.

As a consequence, the
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra
(A,\varphi)
is formal if and only if there is a zig-zag of
\infty
-quasi-isomorphisms linking
(H,\varphi_{*})
and
(H,\varphi_{t})
. By the invertibility of
\infty
-quasi-isomorphisms
[
9
, Theorem 4.18]
, this is equivalent to the existence of a single
\infty
-quasi-isomorphism, which, since
H
has vanishing differential, is given by a
\infty
-isotopy up to an isomorphism of
H
.

An
\infty
-isotopy is an
\infty
-morphism of
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structures whose first component is the identity. Recall the convolution dg Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{H}=\left(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{End}_{H}\right),\partial,\star\right),

whose Maurer–Cartan elements are in bijection with
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structures on
H
. By
[
3
, Proposition 1.1]
,
\varphi_{t}
and
\varphi_{*}
are
\infty
-isotopic if and only if the corresponding Maurer–Cartan elements in
\mathfrak{g}_{H}
, also denoted
\varphi_{*}
and
\varphi_{t}
, are gauge-equivalent. Putting together all of these results, we have the following characterization of formality and intrinsic formality.

Proposition 3.15
.

A
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra
(A,\varphi)
is formal if and only if
\varphi_{*}
and
\varphi_{t}
are gauge equivalent. A
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
(H,\psi)
is intrinsically formal if and only if
\psi
is rigid, in the sense of
Definition

3.11
.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{C}
is a reduced weight-graded coproperad (with no differential) and let
H
be a graded
R
-module. Then, the associated convolution dg Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{H}
is a weight-graded Lie algebra in the sense of
Assumptions

1
(1), with the weight grading coming from that of
\mathcal{C}
, and associated filtration
\mathcal{F}^{N}\mathfrak{g}_{H}
. The dg Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{H}
always has an extra filtration where
\mathcal{L}^{i}\mathfrak{g}_{H}
is all the operations with
(i+1)
or more inputs. More precisely, the
(\mathbb{S}^{op}\times\mathbb{S})
-module
\mathcal{C}
has a direct sum decomposition under a
second
grading

\mathcal{C}=I\oplus\mathcal{C}_{(1)}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{(2)}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{(3)}\oplus\dots

where
\mathcal{C}_{(\ell)}
is spanned by the operations with
\ell
outputs. This gives a decomposition

\mathfrak{g}_{H}=(\mathfrak{g}_{H})_{(1)}\times(\mathfrak{g}_{H})_{(2)}\times(\mathfrak{g}_{H})_{(3)}\times\dots

and we can then define the extra filtration by

\mathcal{L}^{i}\mathfrak{g}_{H}=\prod_{\ell\geqslant i+1}(\mathfrak{g}_{H})_{(\ell)}.

Theorem 3.16
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a reduced weight-graded coproperad, such that the extra filtration
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{H}
is bounded with respect to
\mathcal{F}
, satisfying (2) of
Assumptions

1
. Suppose that
(H,\gamma)
be an
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structure on a graded
R
-module
H
. The following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

There exists an
\infty
-quasi-isomorphism between
(H,\gamma)
and
\left(H,\gamma^{(1)}\right)

(2)

The Maurer–Cartan elements
\gamma
and
\gamma^{(1)}
are gauge equivalent in
\mathfrak{g}_{H}
.

(3)

The gauge-triviality degree of
\phi=\gamma-\gamma^{(1)}
is equal to
\infty
.

(4)

For all
k\geqslant 1
, the
k
-th intermediate gauge triviality degree of
\phi
is defined and equal to
\infty
.

Proof.

The equivalence
(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)
is
[
3
, Proposition 1.1]
, and the remaining equivalences are given by
Theorem

3.10
.
∎

When
\gamma
and
\gamma^{(1)}
are respectively the transferred and induced structures
\varphi_{t}
and
\varphi_{*}
on the homology of a dg
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra
(A,\varphi)
, one can then apply the theorem above to characterize the formality of
\varphi
. Similarly, we can use the rigidity criteria developed in the previous subsection to characterize intrinsic formality. Combining
Proposition

3.14
and
Proposition

3.15
, we have the following result.

Theorem 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a reduced weight-graded coproperad, such that the extra filtration
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{H}
is bounded with respect to
\mathcal{F}
, satisfying (2) of
Assumptions

1
, and
(H,\psi)
be an
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-algebra structure concentrated in weight one (that is, a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra structure), which is
\mathcal{L}^{0}
-rigid. If the image of the map

\mathcal{F}^{2}H_{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi})\longrightarrow H_{-1}(\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}/\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi})

vanishes for every
i\geqslant 1
, then
(H,\psi)
is intrinsically formal.

4.
Coformality of based loop spaces of spheres

In this section, we use the formalism explained in the previous sections to study formality of
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}_{\infty}
-algebra structures on the homology algebra of based loop spaces of spheres. That is, we consider
\Omega\mathcal{C}
-structures where
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{Y}^{(n){\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\antishrieksymbol$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\antishrieksymbol$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\antishrieksymbol$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\antishrieksymbol$}}}}
; for any chain complex
(A,d_{A})
, the corresponding convolution dg Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{A}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{A}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\geqslant 2}\mathrm{Hom}(A[1]^{\otimes i},A)[1]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{ij\neq 0}\mathrm{Hom}(A[1]^{\otimes i}\otimes A[1]^{\otimes j},A\otimes A)^{(2,n)}[n-1]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\ell\geqslant 3}CH^{*}_{(\ell)}(A)^{(\ell,n)}[(n-2)(\ell-1)+1]\ .

Recall that the superscript
(-)^{(\ell,n)}
denotes the subcomplex of elements that have the appropriate cyclic symmetry. The dg Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{A}
is the dg Lie subalgebra of
CH^{*}_{[n]}(A)[1]
of operations with total arity (number of in- and outputs) greater than or equal to
3
, endowed with the necklace bracket, which we will just denote by
[-,-]
. The weight grading is given by the
total number of legs
minus two. That is, the weight of a map
\varphi:A^{\otimes N_{\mathrm{in}}}\to A^{\otimes N_{\mathrm{out}}}
is

\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}\nolimits(\varphi)=N_{\mathrm{in}}+N_{\mathrm{out}}-2\ .

The extra filtration
\mathcal{L}
is bounded with respect to
\mathcal{F}
, since
\mathcal{L}^{i}
is spanned by all operations with at least
(i+1)
-outputs, so for any
k
we have

\mathcal{L}^{k+1}\mathfrak{g}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{k}\mathfrak{g}_{A},

and
\mathcal{L}
satisfies (2) of
Assumptions

1
. We note that the differential on every graded piece
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ell-1}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}_{A}
is given by taking necklace bracket with the
A_{\infty}
-structure
\mu
, which is the usual differential on
CH^{*}_{(\ell)}(A)
. In other words, unraveling all the degree shifts, we find that for every
\ell\geqslant 3
we have an isomorphism of complexes

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ell-1}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}_{A}\cong CH^{-*+(n-2)(\ell-1)+1}_{(\ell)}(A)^{(\ell,n)}

and for
\ell=2
we have an injection

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ell-1}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}_{A}\hookrightarrow CH^{-*+(n-2)(\ell-1)+1}_{(\ell)}(A)^{(\ell,n)}

given by the inclusion of elements of total arity
\geqslant 3
.

Note that
\mathcal{L}^{0}\mathfrak{g}_{A}/\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathfrak{g}_{A}
is nothing but the convolution algebra governing
A_{\infty}
-algebras on
(A,d_{A})
, since it has exactly the operations with one output.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(H,\psi)
be a graded
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra, and
(H,\psi_{(1)})
the corresponding associative algebra. If
(H,\psi_{(1)})
is intrinsically formal as an associative algebra, then
(H,\psi)
is
\mathcal{L}^{0}
-rigid, in the sense of
Definition

3.13
.

Proof.

Let
\psi+\phi
be a deformation of
\psi
with
\phi\in\mathcal{F}^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}
. We decompose

\phi=\phi_{(1)}+\phi_{(2)}+\dots

where
\phi_{(\ell)}
has
\ell
outputs. Note that
(\psi_{(1)}+\phi_{(1)})
must be an
A_{\infty}
-structure. If
\psi_{(1)}
is intrinsically formal as an associative algebra, then there is a gauge transformation trivializing
\phi_{1}
; so applying it to all of
\phi
we get a gauge transformation sending it into an element in
\mathcal{L}^{2}\mathfrak{g}_{H}
.
∎

We now turn to the specific example of based loop spaces of spheres. Recall the definition of coformality for a local Poincaré duality pair
(X,[X])
, given in
Definition

2.34
.

Theorem 4.2
.

For any
n\geqslant 1
, the pair
(S^{n},[S^{n}])
is intrinsically coformal.

Proof.

Let us first present the case
n\geqslant 2
. The relevant homology algebra is

H=H_{*}(\Omega S^{n})\cong R[t]\ ,

where
t
has degree
(n-1)
. This is a smooth
n
-Calabi–Yau algebra, with weak CY structure represented by
\omega=1[t]\in CH_{n}(H)
and compatible
n
-pre-CY structure given by

\psi=\mu+\alpha,\quad\mu\in CH^{2}(H),\quad\alpha\in CH^{n}_{(2)}(H),

where
\mu
is the usual multiplication on
H
, see
[
22
, Proposition 7.1]
.
Since
\psi
is a
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structure, it has homogeneous weight one in
\mathfrak{g}_{H}
, and in terms of the extra filtration
\mathcal{L}
we have

\mu\in\mathcal{L}^{0}\mathfrak{g}_{H}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathfrak{g}_{H}\ .

First we note that
H
is an intrinsically formal associative algebra, so due to
Proposition

4.1

\psi
is
\mathcal{L}^{0}
-rigid. Therefore, by
Theorem

3.17
, we just have to prove that the image of the maps

\mathcal{F}^{2}H_{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}\mathfrak{g}_{H}^{\psi})\to H_{-1}(\mathfrak{g}_{H}^{\psi}/\mathcal{L}^{i+1}\mathfrak{g}_{H}^{\psi})

vanish for each
i\geqslant 1
. The graded pieces of the
\mathcal{L}
filtration, with the induced differential, give subcomplexes

\mathcal{F}^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ell-1}\mathfrak{g}_{H}^{\psi}\subset\left(CH^{*+n\ell-n-2\ell+3}_{(\ell)}(H),[\mu,-]\right)

spanned by the vertices that are cyclically (anti)symmetric and have 4 total legs or more. Note that here we changed the index using
\ell=i+1
so that
\ell
denotes the number of output legs. In order to understand the cohomology of the right-hand side of the inclusion above, we use the fact that the elements
\alpha
and
\omega
are inverses of each other, in the sense of
[
12
]
; they give chain equivalences between
CH_{*}(H)
and all the complexes
CH^{*}_{(\ell)}(H)
, up to appropriate shifts. Let us be more precise: for any
\ell\geqslant 1
, there is an explicit quasi-isomorphism

g_{(\ell)}\colon CH_{*}(H)\xrightarrow{\sim}CH^{-*+n\ell}_{(\ell)}(H)

given by evaluating a certain diagram on some Hochschild chain
x
. For example, when
\ell=3
this diagram is

x
\alpha
\alpha
\alpha
1
2
3

The diagrams for other values are similar, with
\ell
copies of the
\alpha
vertex around the circle. We now recall the Hochschild homology of
H
, in other words the homology of the free loop space
\Lambda S^{n}
, together with a choice of representative for the generator of each factor. When
n
is odd, we have:

\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccccccccccc}HH_{*}(H)&\cong&R&\oplus&R[n-1]&\oplus&R[n]&\oplus&R[2n-2]&\oplus&R[2n-1]&\oplus\cdots\\
&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}\\
&&1&&t&&1[t]&&t^{2}&&t[t]&\end{array}

and when
n
is even, we have

\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccccccccccc}HH_{*}(H)&\cong&R&\oplus&R[n-1]&\oplus&R[n]&\oplus&R[3n-3]&\oplus&R[3n-2]&\oplus\cdots\\
&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}\\
&&1&&t&&1[t]&&t^{3}&&t^{2}[t]&\end{array}

Let us study the cyclic symmetry properties of the elements in
CH^{*}_{(\ell)}(H)
that are images of the representatives above. Let us denote by

\rho\colon CH^{*}_{(\ell)}(H)\to CH^{*}_{(\ell)}(H)

the rotation automorphism; for an element
\xi
to have the appropriate symmetry we must have
\rho(\xi)=(-1)^{(n-1)(\ell-1)}\xi
. Using a similar diagram to the above, and using the sign calculus explained in
[
11
]
, we find a homotopy between

g_{(\ell)}\quad\text{and}\quad(-1)^{(n-1)(\ell-1)}\rho\circ g_{(\ell)}.

Since
R
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra, we can symmetrize, and for any
k
and any closed element
x
of
CH_{*}(H)
we use the homotopy above to conclude that there is a homology equivalence

g_{(\ell)}(x)\simeq\frac{1}{\ell}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}(-1)^{i(n-1)(\ell-1)}\rho^{i}(g_{(\ell)}(x))\right),

and therefore all the classes
[g_{(\ell)}(x)]
can be represented by appropriately symmetric elements. Using the explicit formula for
\alpha
and for the map
g_{(\ell)}
, we calculate that, for any
\ell
and
k
,

(1)

the element
g_{(\ell)}(1[t^{k+1}])
is already appropriately symmetric on the nose, and cohomologous to
(k+1)g_{(\ell)}(t^{k}[t])
, since
1[t^{k+1}]
is homologous to
(k+1)t^{k}[t]
, and

(2)

the following equation holds

\left[\alpha,\frac{1}{\ell-1}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\rho^{i}(-1)^{i(n-1)(\ell-2)}(g_{(\ell-1)}(t^{k+1}))\right)\right]=g_{(\ell)}(1[t^{k+1}]).

By counting degrees, we know that any homology class of
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ell-1}\mathfrak{g}_{H}^{\psi}
in degree
-1
must be the image of a class of degree
(n+2\ell-4)
in
HH_{*}(H)
under the map
g_{(\ell)}
, thus a class whose homological degree has the same parity as
n
. These are exactly the classes represented by a multiple of
t^{k}[t]
for some
k
. But the differential on
\mathfrak{g}^{\psi}_{H}
is given by taking commutator with
\psi=\mu+\alpha
, so
(1)
and
(2)
above imply that all these classes
g_{(\ell)}(t^{k}[t])
vanish in the relevant group

H_{-1}\left(\mathfrak{g}_{H}^{\psi}/\mathcal{L}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}_{H}^{\psi}\right),

and so we can apply
Theorem

3.17
.

In the remaining case
n=1
, where
H=k[t,t^{-1}]
, with
\deg(t)=0
. Here the compatible
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structures are of the form
\psi=\mu+\alpha+\tau
,
where
\tau
is some element with zero inputs and three outputs, see for example the structure in
[
22
, Section 7.4]
. There will be many compatible
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
-algebra structures, as the dimension condition in
Proposition

2.35
fails. Nevertheless, the same argument we presented above in the
n\geq 2
case applies to any such structure, but even more simply. Since
HH_{*}(H)
is concentrated in degrees zero and one when
n=1
, there can only be nontrivial classes of degree
(n+2\ell-4)
in
HH_{*}(H)
when
\ell=2
. So the only classes in homological degree
-1
in
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ell-1}\mathfrak{g}_{H}^{\psi}
are multiples of the classes
g_{(2)}(t^{k}[t])
, but all of these are weight one. We conclude that
\mathcal{F}^{2}H_{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ell-1}\mathfrak{g}_{H}^{\psi})
vanishes for all
\ell\geq 2
and we can again apply
Theorem

3.17
.
∎
